```json
  "report": "\n# Reviewer 1 (R1): Methods and Experiments\n\n## Paper: \"Depth Fingerprinting: Detecting Recursive Generation Depth in LLM Text Chains\"\n\n---\n\n## 1. Methodological Soundness\n\n**Overall assessment: Generally rigorous with some notable design concerns.**\n\n**Strengths of experimental design:**\n\n- **LOGO protocol** is well-chosen and correctly implemented. Holding out entire generators (rather than random splits) is the right way to evaluate cross-generator generalization. The paper correctly identifies that gen-0 seed sharing inflates 3-class LOGO and introduces leak-free binary F1 as the primary metric — this is methodologically sound and self-aware.\n- **LOFO (Leave-One-Family-Out)** is an important complement to LOGO, since LOGO does not isolate family effects (e.g., Pythia-1B held out while Pythia-2.8B remains in training). LOFO binary F1 = 0.686 confirms cross-family transfer.\n- **Multiple effect-size metrics** (Cohen's d, Glass's Delta, Cliff's delta, Spearman rho, KS statistics) provide convergent evidence for the DDR partition. This is good practice when Levene tests reject homoscedasticity.\n- **Independent-seed control** (Appendix F) directly addresses the most obvious confound (shared Wikipedia seeds) and finds only a 1.4 pp gap, which is within bootstrap CIs.\n\n**Design concerns:**\n\n- **Generator subset inconsistency**: The paper uses at least 5 different generator subsets across experiments (5-gen, 6-gen, 8-gen, 9-gen, and various 3-4 gen subsets). Table in Appendix (generator subsets) documents this honestly, but it makes cross-experiment comparison difficult. The cross-model chain result (binary F1 = 0.830) uses a 5-gen subset and is \"not directly comparable\" to the 8-gen LOGO (0.694). This heterogeneity, while explained as chronological expansion, weakens the narrative coherence.\n- **Single reference model**: Pythia-410M is used throughout. While cross-reference validation (Table in Appendix, spread 0.013) is reassuring, it uses only 3 references and only on the 5-gen subset. The Pythia-family affinity concern (Pythia generators achieving highest F1) is acknowledged but not fully resolved.\n- **Prompt type evaluation on single generator**: Prompt-type analysis (Appendix C) uses only Pythia-2.8B. This is a significant limitation — prompt-type sensitivity could interact with model family, and single-generator results cannot establish generality.\n- **Cross-domain temperature confound**: Cross-domain experiments (Appendix J) used t=1.0, which is outside the method's reliable operating range. The paper acknowledges this but the confound means domain generalization is not cleanly established.\n\n---\n\n## 2. Experimental Coverage\n\nThe paper is remarkably thorough in its experimental coverage. Mapping against standard reviewer expectations for this type of work:\n\n**Covered comprehensively (with dedicated tables/figures):**\n1. Core classification performance (Table 1 / 8-gen LOGO) ✓\n2. Feature ablation / complementarity (Table 2, Appendix) ✓\n3. DDR characterization with multiple effect sizes (Section 4.2) ✓\n4. Baseline comparison (Table 3) ✓\n5. Cross-model chains (Section 4.1, Appendix) ✓\n6. Cross-reference models (Appendix) ✓\n7. Temperature sensitivity (Appendix) ✓\n8. Cross-temperature transfer (Appendix) ✓\n9. Length confound control (Appendix B) ✓\n10. 5-class finer-grained evaluation (Appendix D) ✓\n11. QLoRA end-to-end (Appendix E) ✓\n12. LOFO feature analysis (Appendix F) ✓\n13. Confusion matrix analysis (Appendix G) ✓\n14. Alternative classifiers + ordinal methods (Appendix H) ✓\n15. Human edit robustness (Appendix I) ✓\n16. Cross-domain (Appendix J) ✓\n17. Instruction-tuned models (Appendix K) ✓\n18. Error analysis (Appendix L) ✓\n19. Downstream utility (Appendix M) ✓\n20. Seed control (Appendix) ✓\n21. TOST equivalence testing (Appendix) ✓\n22. DDR causality — Granger + permutation importance (Appendix) ✓\n23. 9-gen scale evaluation (Appendix) ✓\n24. Transfer matrix (Appendix) ✓\n25. Prompt-type generalization (Appendix C) ✓\n\n**Notable gaps / missing experiments:**\n1. **No non-English evaluation**: All seeds are English Wikipedia. The paper acknowledges this in Limitations but provides no multilingual data point whatsoever.\n2. **No frontier-scale models (70B+)**: The largest model is 8B. This is noted in Limitations but is a significant gap given practical relevance.\n3. **No real-world contamination evaluation**: All chains are synthetic/controlled. No evaluation on naturally-occurring recursive generation (e.g., texts from web crawls where depth is independently verified).\n4. **No paraphrastic chain comparison in same framework**: While the paper cites concurrent work on paraphrastic chains, a direct within-framework comparison (using the same features on paraphrastic vs. non-paraphrastic chains) would strengthen the positioning. The paper mentions \"paraphrastic: 0.62 vs non-paraphrastic: 0.781\" in the summary but I could not find a dedicated table for this comparison in the paper body.\n5. **Limited prompt diversity**: Only 3 prompt types tested, and only on Pythia-2.8B. Missing: summarization, instruction following, question answering — prompt types that are more representative of real-world usage.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Statistical Rigor\n\n**Bootstrap CIs**: Correctly applied throughout (n=1,000 or n=2,000 as noted). Percentile bootstrap is appropriate for non-parametric F1 distributions.\n\n**TOST equivalence testing**: \n- Appropriately used for the downstream utility null result (Appendix M) and DDR stationarity (Appendix).\n- The choice of d=0.20 (Cohen's small) as SESOI for feature stationarity is justified via LOFO analysis — features below this threshold change F1 by ≤1.5 pp. This is a strong justification.\n- Sensitivity analysis across epsilon={0.15, 0.20, 0.25} is good practice.\n- **Concern**: The downstream TOST uses only n=3 seeds. The paper correctly flags this (\"preliminary observation rather than a definitive null result\"), but the Cohen's d values of 86.0 and 119.7 are indeed meaningless with 3 seeds. The TOST conclusion, while technically valid, generalizes over extremely limited variation.\n- Bootstrap TOST stability (1,000 iterations, 100% rejection rate for near-stationary features) is a strong additional validation.\n\n**Granger causality**: Applied with Fisher combination across 8 generators, lag=1. The lag=1 restriction (due to T=5 time points in the chain) is a hard constraint that limits what can be concluded about longer-range dependencies. The paper should note that Granger causality with only T=5 observations per chain has very limited power — the significant results come from the large N (29,095 chains) rather than long temporal sequences.\n\n**Bonferroni correction**: Applied appropriately within test families (21 distributional comparisons, 4 baseline comparisons). Correctly scoped.\n\n**McNemar tests**: Used for pairwise comparisons (feature ablation, baseline comparisons). Appropriate for paired classification outcomes.\n\n**Potential issues:**\n- The paper reports \"all p ≈ 0 after Bonferroni correction\" for KS tests — with N=37,058, virtually any distributional difference will be significant. The paper correctly supplements with effect sizes (d, rho) which are more informative, but the KS p-values add little information.\n- Class-balanced subsampling is mentioned for feature-depth correlations but the specific balancing procedure (random downsampling? stratified?) is not detailed.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Strengths\n\n**S1. Novel and well-motivated task formulation.** Generation depth classification (gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+) is a genuinely new task that fills a gap between binary AI detection and source attribution. The connection to data provenance and model collapse is well-articulated. The convergence with concurrent work (Broken Telephone, Markovian Chains) on ~3 distinguishable classes strengthens the contribution.\n\n**S2. Exceptional experimental thoroughness.** 24+ distinct experiments across 11 main-text and 13+ appendix tables. The paper systematically addresses virtually every standard reviewer concern (length confound, seed leakage, classifier dependence, alternative metrics, prompt sensitivity, temperature sensitivity, cross-domain, instruction tuning). The negative downstream finding (Claim 3) is honestly reported rather than buried. The generator subset table (Appendix) transparently documents which experiments use which subsets.\n\n**S3. The DDR partition is convincingly established.** Five independent effect-size metrics (rho, d, Glass's Delta, Cliff's delta, KS) converge on the same 3/1/3 partition. Data-driven clustering (k=2, silhouette=0.632) recovers the primary split. Holdout generator splits confirm stability. TOST bootstrap (100% rejection, 1,000 iterations) confirms near-stationary equivalence. Per-generator TOST provides individual-level validation. This is among the most thorough feature-partition validations I have seen in an empirical NLP paper.\n\n**S4. Honest scope delimitation.** The paper does not overclaim. Primary metric is leak-free binary F1 (0.694), not the inflated 3-class (0.762). Instruction-tuning degradation is prominently discussed. The 7-feature ensemble is positioned on efficiency, not absolute performance (multi-detector ensemble achieves 0.708 vs 0.705 at 2.4x cost). The downstream null result is framed as \"diagnostic, not curative.\" Temperature boundaries are clearly stated.\n\n**S5. Interpretability of the approach.** Using 7 handcrafted features rather than learned representations enables the DDR decomposition, which is the paper's primary intellectual contribution. The location/shape distinction provides a mechanistic hypothesis (softmax + fixed sampling preserves shape, successive conditioning compresses location) that is testable and connects to model collapse theory.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Weaknesses\n\n**W1. Practical applicability is severely limited by base-model restriction.** The paper's primary scope limitation — base models only — is honestly acknowledged but fundamentally constrains impact. Real-world LLM text is overwhelmingly produced by instruction-tuned/RLHF models. OLMo-Instruct LOGO F1 drops to 0.338 (vs 0.763 base); cross-generator instruct LOGO is only 0.487. While QLoRA recovers signal on instruct data (0.89-0.91), this is within-model CV only, not cross-generator. The paper's core contribution (DDR as a property of recursive generation) may not hold under instruction tuning, since the feature-level partition collapses (silhouette drops from 0.632 to 0.338). This is not just a scope limitation — it raises the question of whether DDR is an artifact of base-model sampling regularities that instruction tuning disrupts.\n\n**W2. Generator subset heterogeneity weakens comparability.** At least 5 different generator subsets are used across experiments (5-gen cross-model, 6-gen QLoRA/LOFO/temp, 8-gen main LOGO, 9-gen scale, various 3-4 gen subsets for domain/instruct). While the paper documents this via the generator subset table and explains it as chronological expansion, it means that numbers from different experiments cannot be directly compared. The headline cross-model result (0.830) uses the 5-gen subset, the primary LOGO (0.694) uses 8-gen, QLoRA (0.905) uses 6-gen, and 9-gen includes Gemma. A reader cannot easily assess whether improvements come from the method or the generator subset.\n\n**W3. Cross-domain evaluation is confounded by temperature.** The cross-domain experiment (Appendix J) used t=1.0, top-p=1.0 — outside the method's established reliable operating range (t ≤ 0.7). The paper notes a \"domain x temperature interaction\" but cannot cleanly separate domain generalization from temperature effects. The supplementary t=0.7 evaluation shows substantial variation (Academic domain binary F1 = 0.628 at t=0.7), suggesting that cross-domain generalization may be weak under standard conditions. This undercuts claims about domain robustness.\n\n**W4. The \"location shift\" mechanistic explanation remains speculative.** The paper proposes that DDR reflects \"softmax architecture and fixed sampling parameters constraining distributional shape under standard decoding regimes, while each successive generation compresses the location toward the model's preferred output distribution.\" This is framed as a hypothesis but discussed as if established. The Granger causality analysis (6/7 significant) establishes temporal predictiveness, not mechanism. The paper would benefit from a more careful distinction between the empirical DDR observation (well-established) and the proposed mechanism (speculative). The collapse of DDR at t=1.0 and under instruction tuning is consistent with multiple alternative explanations.\n\n**W5. Downstream utility experiment is underpowered.** Only 3 seeds per condition, single evaluation metric (perplexity), single downstream model (OLMo-2-0425-1B), single task (CLM). Cohen's d values of 86-120 are acknowledged as meaningless. The TOST at ±2% margin with n=3 is technically valid but provides very weak evidence for generalization. The paper correctly calls this \"preliminary,\" but it is presented as Claim 3 — a core contribution. A core negative claim deserves stronger evidence than n=3 seeds.\n\n---\n\n## 6. Questions for Authors\n\n**Q1.** The DDR partition collapses under instruction tuning (silhouette 0.632 → 0.338). Does this mean DDR is a property of base-model sampling regularities rather than a fundamental property of recursive generation? If so, how should the contribution be reframed?\n\n**Q2.** The cross-model chain result (binary F1 = 0.830) is substantially higher than single-generator LOGO (0.694). You attribute this to \"generator heterogeneity amplifying depth-correlated distributional shifts.\" Could an alternative explanation be that generator identity is acting as an additional signal (different generators at different depths create distributional differences beyond depth)? The single-generator transfer analysis (Appendix, mean F1 = 0.810) helps but does not fully rule this out since it also uses a binary (not 3-class) objective.\n\n**Q3.** Tail decay passes TOST equivalence for only 4/8 generators (Table in Appendix). The 4 non-passing generators show |d| = 0.15-0.22, which overlaps with the intermediate zone. Should tail decay be reclassified as intermediate rather than near-stationary? What impact would this have on the DDR narrative?\n\n**Q4.** The prompt-type analysis uses only Pythia-2.8B. Have you considered whether the continuation > elaboration ordering holds for non-Pythia generators? This is important because the strength of DDR could be conflated with model-specific generation properties.\n\n**Q5.** For the Gemma-2-2B anomaly (gen-1 F1 = 0.036), you hypothesize that \"first-generation Gemma continuations occupy a region of feature space highly similar to human-written text.\" Have you verified this by examining the actual feature distributions? Is it possible that the Gemma tokenizer mismatch with the Pythia-410M reference model is the primary cause?\n\n---\n\n## 7. Minor Issues\n\n1. **Table numbering confusion**: The paper summary mentions Tables 1-11 in the main text and Tables 12-24 in the appendix, but the actual paper uses section-based referencing for many appendix tables. Cross-referencing between the task description and the actual paper is difficult.\n\n2. **Inconsistent F1 values across tables**: The feature ablation appears in both the main text (Section 4.2: \"F1 = 0.773\") and Appendix (Table: \"F1 = 0.773\"), using 8-gen 10-fold CV. But the LOFO appendix reports \"full = 0.789\" using 6-gen LOGO. These use different protocols and subsets, which could confuse readers who compare numbers across sections.\n\n3. **\"Entropy\" terminology**: The paper acknowledges using \"entropy\" as shorthand for token-level NLL (Section 3.3), which is non-standard. This is fine but should be flagged more prominently — possibly in the abstract or introduction — since the DDR characterization is built on these features.\n\n4. **Missing detail on class-balanced subsampling**: The evaluation protocol mentions class-balanced subsamples but does not specify the procedure (random downsampling to minimum class? repeated subsampling?). This affects reproducibility.\n\n5. **Cross-reference table placement**: Table 4 (cross-reference models) is buried deep in the appendix rather than in the main text, despite being referenced in Section 4.2. Given that reference-model dependence is a key concern, this table deserves main-text placement.\n\n6. **Confusion between table1.tex/table2.tex and inline tables**: table1.tex is marked \"NOT \\input'd by main.tex\" — the actual tables are inline in results.tex. This is confusing for the supplementary materials but does not affect the paper itself.\n\n7. **OLMo version discrepancy**: The temperature experiments note that OLMo-2-0425-1B (April 2025 release) is used instead of the original OLMo-2-1B-hf. This model substitution should be more prominently flagged since it introduces a potential confound in cross-experiment comparisons.\n\n---\n\n## 8. Score\n\n**Score: 3.5 (Borderline Conference / Strong Findings)**\n\n**Justification:**\n\nThe paper makes a genuine contribution through (a) a novel and well-motivated task formulation (generation depth classification), (b) a thorough empirical characterization of DDR across 8 generators from 5 families, and (c) an exceptionally comprehensive experimental appendix that systematically addresses standard reviewer concerns. The DDR partition is convincingly established through multiple convergent metrics, and the paper demonstrates unusual honesty in scope delimitation.\n\nHowever, practical impact is severely limited by the base-model restriction. The paper's central finding — that DDR enables depth classification — applies to a setting (base-model text chains) that has limited real-world prevalence. The collapse of DDR structure under instruction tuning (silhouette 0.632 → 0.338) raises questions about whether DDR is a fundamental property of recursive generation or an artifact of base-model sampling. The cross-domain evaluation is confounded by temperature, the downstream utility experiment is underpowered (n=3 seeds), and the generator subset heterogeneity makes cross-experiment comparison difficult.\n\nFor a top venue (EMNLP Main): the limited practical scope and the unresolved question about DDR under instruction tuning weigh against acceptance. For Findings: the novel task formulation, thorough characterization, and honest reporting of scope boundaries make this a solid empirical contribution. The paper is well-written, statistically careful, and fills a genuine gap between binary detection and source attribution.\n\n---\n\n## 9. Confidence\n\n**Confidence: 4/5**\n\nI have read the full paper including all appendices and supporting materials in detail. I am familiar with the detection, model collapse, and iterative generation literatures. I am confident in my assessment of methodological soundness and statistical rigor. My slight uncertainty stems from: (a) I have not independently verified the statistical claims, and (b) the instruction-tuning question (W1) could have a satisfactory answer that I am not seeing from the current evidence.\n"
}
```

Review submitted. Here is a summary of my assessment:

**Score: 3.5 (Borderline Conference / Strong Findings) | Confidence: 4/5**

**Key Strengths:**
- Novel task formulation (generation depth classification beyond binary detection)
- DDR partition convincingly established through 5 convergent effect-size metrics, data-driven clustering, holdout validation, and TOST bootstrap
- Exceptional experimental thoroughness: 24+ experiments across 11 main-text and 13+ appendix tables, systematically addressing standard reviewer concerns
- Honest scope delimitation: leak-free binary F1 as primary metric, instruction-tuning degradation prominently discussed, negative downstream finding reported transparently

**Key Weaknesses:**
- **W1**: Base-model-only restriction severely limits practical impact. DDR structure collapses under instruction tuning (silhouette 0.632 to 0.338), raising questions about whether DDR is fundamental to recursion or an artifact of base-model sampling
- **W2**: Generator subset heterogeneity (5 different subsets across experiments) makes cross-experiment comparison difficult
- **W3**: Cross-domain evaluation confounded by temperature (used t=1.0, outside reliable range)
- **W4**: "Location shift" mechanism remains speculative despite being presented prominently
- **W5**: Downstream utility claim (Claim 3) underpowered with only n=3 seeds

**Critical Questions:**
1. Does DDR collapse under instruction tuning mean it is a base-model artifact rather than a recursive generation property?
2. Is the cross-model chain F1 boost (0.830 vs 0.694) partly driven by generator identity as an additional signal?
3. Should tail_decay (TOST passes only 4/8 generators) be reclassified as intermediate?
